, if in state 2 and 3, the TMNU
{\bf M}
updates its tape and neural dimensions 1 and 2 exactly as
{\bf N}
would in states 1 and 2, respectively, and that when
{\bf N}
halts, i.e., reaches state 3,
{\bf M}
transitions to state 4. Such a behavior can be expressed formally by imposing some relation between the transition and command functions of both TMNUs.
The following definition makes this relation precise.

{definition}
(Subroutine)
Let
{\bf M}:=(n_{\bf M},d_{\bf M},\delta_{\bf M},\kappa_{\bf M})
and
{\bf N}:=(n_{\bf N},d_{\bf N},\delta_{\bf N},\kappa_{\bf N})
be two TMNUs, such that
n_{\bf N}\leq n_{\bf M}
and
d_{\bf N}\leq d_{\bf M}
, and let
\iota_{q}:\{1,\ldots,n_{\bf N}\}\hookrightarrow\{1,\ldots,n_{\bf M}\}
and
\iota_{\omega}:\{1,\ldots,d_{\bf N}\}\hookrightarrow\{1,\ldots,d_{\bf M}\}
be two injective functions. We say that
{\bf N}
is a
\newname
subroutine of
{\bf M}
with correspondance
(\iota_{q},\iota_{\omega})
if for every
q\in\{1,\ldots,n_{\bf N}-1\}
,
\sigma\in\Gamma
and
\omega\in\R^{d_{\bf M}}
, we have

\delta_{\bf M}(\iota_{q}(q),\sigma)=(\iota_{q}\circ\delta^{q}_{\bf N}(q,\sigma),\delta^{\sigma}_{\bf N}(q,\sigma),\delta^{m}_{\bf N}(q,\sigma)),

(129)

\pi_{\iota_{\omega}}\kappa_{\bf M}(\iota_{q}(q),\sigma)(\omega)=\kappa_{\bf N}(q,\sigma)(\pi_{\iota_{\omega}}\omega),\quad\text{and}\quad\pi^{\perp}_{\iota_{\omega}}\kappa_{\bf M}(\iota_{q}(q),\sigma)(\omega)=\pi^{\perp}_{\iota_{\omega}}\omega,

(130)

where
\pi_{\iota_{\omega}}:\R^{d_{\bf M}}\to\R^{d_{\bf N}}
is the projection defined by
\pi_{\iota_{\omega}}\omega=(\omega_{i})_{i\in\iota_{\omega}(\{1,\ldots,d_{\bf N}\})}
, and
\pi^{\perp}_{\iota_{\omega}}:\R^{d_{\bf M}}\to\R^{d_{\bf M}-d_{\bf N}}
is the orthogonal projection defined by
\pi^{\perp}_{\iota_{\omega}}\omega=(\omega_{i})_{i\notin\iota_{\omega}(\{1,\ldots,d_{\bf N}\})}
.

We now introduce two pieces of notation that make the bookkeeping in subroutine arguments more transparent. Let
{\bf N}
be a subroutine of
{\bf M}
with correspondence
(\iota_{q},\iota_{\omega})
. If
c=(q;\tau;\omega)
is a configuration of
{\bf M}
with
q\in\iota_{q}(\{1,\ldots,n_{\bf N}\})
, we define its
\newname
subroutine shadow by

\operatorname{Sh}_{\iota_{q},\iota_{\omega}}(c):=\left(\iota_{q}^{-1}(q);\tau;\pi_{\iota_{\omega}}\omega\right).

(131)

Conversely, if
c^{\prime}=(q^{\prime};\tau^{\prime};\omega^{\prime})
is a configuration of
{\bf N}
and
h^{\perp}\in\R^{d_{\bf M}-d_{\bf N}}
, we define the
\newname
lift of
c^{\prime}
with frozen complementary neural state
h^{\perp}
as the configuration

\operatorname{Lift}_{\iota_{q},\iota_{\omega}}^{h^{\perp}}(c^{\prime}):=(q;\tau;\omega),

(132)

where

q:=\iota_{q}(q^{\prime}),\quad\tau:=\tau^{\prime},\quad\pi_{\iota_{\omega}}\omega:=\omega^{\prime},\quad\text{and}\quad\pi^{\perp}_{\iota_{\omega}}\omega:=h^{\perp}.

(133)

The subroutine lemma states that the large machine follows the shadow computation exactly until the subroutine halts.

{lemma}

Let
{\bf M},{\bf N}
be two TMNUs such that
{\bf N}
is a subroutine of
{\bf M}
with correspondance
(\iota_{q},\iota_{\omega})
. Let
c:=(q;\tau;\omega)
be a configuration of
{\bf M}
such that
q\in\iota_{q}(\{1,\ldots,n_{\bf N}-1\})
, and define

c^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Sh}_{\iota_{q},\iota_{\omega}}(c),\quad\text{and}\quad h^{\perp}:=\pi^{\perp}_{\iota_{\omega}}\omega.

(134)

Then, for all
t\in\{0,\ldots,T_{\bf N}(c^{\prime})\}
,

{\bf M}^{t}(c)=\operatorname{Lift}_{\iota_{q},\iota_{\omega}}^{h^{\perp}}\left({\bf N}^{t}(c^{\prime})\right),

(135)

where
T_{\bf N}(c^{\prime})
is the halting time of
c^{\prime}
in
{\bf N}
defined by
T_{\bf N}(c^{\prime}):=\inf\{t\in\No:\pi_{q}{\bf N}^{t}(c^{\prime})=n_{\bf N}\}
. In particular, for all
t\in\{0,\ldots,T_{\bf N}(c^{\prime})\}
, we have

\|c\|_{\bf M}^{t}\leq\|c^{\prime}\|_{\bf N}^{t}\vee\|c\|,

(136)

Proof.

Write
{\bf M}^{t}(c)=(q_{t};\tau_{t};\omega_{t})
and
{\bf N}^{t}(c^{\prime})=(q_{t}^{\prime};\tau_{t}^{\prime};\omega_{t}^{\prime})
. We prove (
135
) by induction on
t
. The case
t=0
follows directly from the definitions of
\operatorname{Sh}
and
\operatorname{Lift}
.

Assume that (
135
) holds for some
t<T_{\bf N}(c^{\prime})
. Equivalently, we have

q_{t}=\iota_{q}(q_{t}^{\prime}),\quad\tau_{t}=\tau_{t}^{\prime},\quad\pi_{\iota_{\omega}}\omega_{t}=\omega_{t}^{\prime},\quad\text{and}\quad\pi^{\perp}_{\iota_{\omega}}\omega_{t}=h^{\perp}.

Since
t<T_{\bf N}(c^{\prime})
, the state
q_{t}^{\prime}
is not the halting state of
{\bf N}
. Therefore, the subroutine identities (
129
) and (
130
) apply to
q_{t}^{\prime}
, and yield

q_{t+1}=\iota_{q}(q_{t+1}^{\prime}),\quad\tau_{t+1}=\tau_{t+1}^{\prime},\quad\pi_{\iota_{\omega}}\omega_{t+1}=\omega_{t+1}^{\prime},\quad\text{and}\quad\pi^{\perp}_{\iota_{\omega}}\omega_{t+1}=h^{\perp}.

This is precisely (
135
) at time
t+1
, and the induction is complete.

Finally, by (
135
), for every
s\in\{0,\ldots,t\}
,

\|{\bf M}^{s}(c)\|\leq\|{\bf N}^{s}(c^{\prime})\|\vee\|h^{\perp}\|_{\infty}\leq\|c^{\prime}\|_{\bf N}^{t}\vee\|c\|.

Taking the maximum over
s=0,\ldots,t
gives (
136
).
∎

We now initiate the construction of TMNUs that can approximate any continuous function
f:[-1,1]\to\R
. The general philosophy guiding the incoming constructions is the following. At its roots, our construction exploits the fact that given a continuous function
f:[-1,1]\to\R
, there exists a sequence of polynomials
(P_{i})_{i\in\No}
with coefficients having an arbitrarily long but finite binary representation that converges to
f
. We will show that there exists a TMNU that, given that its tape is initialized as containing some encoding
u_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
of the coefficients of
P_{i}
for some
i\in\No
, and that its neural state is initialized as containing some input
x\in[-1,1]
, can approximate
P_{i}(x)
, and hence
f(x)
. Then, we will use this TMNU as a subroutine of a larger TMNU that, given that its tape is initialized as containing an infinite sequence of bits
u:=u_{0}u_{1}u_{2}\ldots
that is the result of the concatenation of the encodings
u_{i}
of the coefficients of
P_{i}
for every
i\in\No
, and that its neural state is initialized as containing some input
x\in[-1,1]
, can approximate
f(x)
by successively approximating the polynomials
P_{i}(x)
for every
i\in\No
.

In other words, the TMNU we design will be such that, if some infinite binary sequence
u
is written on its tape, will recognize some finite prefix
u_{0}
of
u
as being some instruction to be executed, will execute this instruction, and delete the prefix
u_{0}
from the tape, and then will repeat this process with the remaining infinite binary sequence on the tape. This way, if
u=u_{0}u_{1}u_{2}\ldots
encodes the coefficients of the polynomials
P_{i}
for every
i\in\No
, then the TMNU will be able to successively approximate
P_{i}(x)
for every
i\in\No
, and therefore to approximate
f(x)
. With this philosophy in mind, we will show that every elementary TMNU that we design will be able to read some finite prefix
p
of the infinite sequence
u
written on its tape, recognize
p
as being some instruction to be executed, execute this instruction, and delete the prefix
p
from the tape. This will be reflected in all the statements we will establish regarding the properties of the TMNUs we will design. In the following, we will first give four examples of simple TMNUs that can be used as building blocks to design more complex TMNUs, and then we will show how to use these simple TMNUs as subroutines to design a TMNU that can approximate any continuous function
f:[-1,1]\to\R
.

C.2
Elementary TMNUs

As a first elementary example of the philosophy described above, we define a TMNU that reads the first bit of the infinite binary sequence
u
written on the tape, and depending on this bit, either multiplies the neural state by
-1
or leaves it unchanged, and then deletes this bit from the tape. This TMNU can be seen as a TMNU that reads some finite prefix
u
of the infinite sequence
u
written on the tape, recognizes
u
as being some instruction to be executed, executes this instruction, and deletes the prefix
u
from the tape, where in this case the instruction is "if the first bit of
u
is
1
, then multiply the neural state by
-1
, otherwise do nothing".

{definition}

We let
{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}
be the TMNU with neural dimension
1
and 2 states, defined by the following procedure.

•

State 1:
Let
b\in\{\square,0,1\}
under scan. Then, write
\square
, move right, update the neural state as
\omega\leftarrow(-1)^{b}\cdot\omega
, and go to State
2
.

•

State 2:
Halt.

Its behavior is immediate from the definition and will be used as a one-step subroutine.

{lemma}

Let
b\in\{0,1\}
,
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
and
x\in\R
. Define the configurations
c:=(1;|bv;x)
and
c^{\prime}:=(2;|v;(-1)^{b}x)
. Then,

{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}(c)=c^{\prime},\quad\text{and}\quad\|c\|_{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}^{1}=|x|.

(137)

Proof.

By definition of
{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}
, we have
{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}(c)=c^{\prime}
. Moreover, we have
\|c\|_{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}^{1}=\|c\|\vee\|c^{\prime}\|=|x|
, which concludes the proof.
∎

As a second example of this philosophy, we design a TMNU that, given that its tape is initialized as containing some finite binary sequence
u=1^{k}0
followed by some infinite binary sequence
u
, and that its neural state is initialized as containing some input
x\in[-1,1]
, will delete
u
while multiplying
x
by
2^{k}
. Essentially, this TMNU acts as follows. As long as it reads a
1
on the tape, it multiplies the neural state by
2
and deletes this
1
from the tape. When it reads a
0
on the tape, it deletes this
0
from the tape and halts. Therefore, if the tape is initialized as containing some finite binary sequence
u=1^{k}0
followed by some infinite binary sequence
u
, and if the neural state is initialized as containing some input
x\in[-1,1]
, then after
k+1
steps, the tape will contain only the infinite binary sequence
u
, and the neural state will contain
2^{k}x
. This TMNU is precisely defined as follows.

{definition}

We let
{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}
be the TMNU with neural dimension
1
and 2 states, defined by the following procedure.

•

State 1.
Let
b\in\{\square,0,1\}
under scan.

i.

If
b\in\{\square,0\}
, write
\square
, move right, and go to State
2
.

ii.

If
b=1
, write
\square
, move right, update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow 2\cdot\omega,

and go back to State 1.

•

State 2.
Halt.

We now establish that
{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}
can be used to multiply its input by
2^{k}
for some
k\in
, by reading some finite binary sequence
u=1^{k}0
on the tape, and deleting it. Note that the proof of this result relies on the Chaining Lemma (Lemma
C
), which allows us to split the evolution from some configuration
c
to some configuration
c^{\prime}
into several sub-evolutions, and to study each of these sub-evolutions separately.
The formal statement keeps track of both the final configuration and the trajectory bound.

{lemma}

Let
k\in\No
,
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
and
x\in\R
. Define the configurations
c:=(1;|1^{k}0v,|;x)
and
c^{\prime}:=(2;|v,|;2^{k}x)
. Then,

{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}^{k+1}(c)=c^{\prime},\quad\|c\|_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}^{k+1}\leq 2^{k}|x|.

(138)

In the above Lemma, the tape in configuration
c
contains the finite binary sequence
1^{k}0
followed by some (potentially) infinite binary sequence
v
, and the neural state in configuration
c
contains some input
x\in\R
. The Lemma states that after
k+1
steps, the tape will contain only the infinite binary sequence
v
, and the neural state will contain
2^{k}x
. In other words, the TMNU
{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}
has read the finite binary sequence
1^{k}0
on the tape, recognized it as being an instruction to be executed, executed this instruction by multiplying the input
x
by
2^{k}
, and deleted the finite binary sequence
1^{k}0
from the tape. This is a very simple example of how a TMNU can read some finite prefix of an infinite binary sequence written on its tape, recognize it as being some instruction to be executed, execute this instruction, and delete this prefix from the tape. We now give the proof of the above Lemma.

Proof.

Let
k\in\No
and
x\in\R
. We define the configurations
c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{k+1}
of
{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}
by

c_{t}:=(1;|1^{k-t}0v,|;2^{t}x),\quad t\in\{0,\ldots,k\},

and

c_{k+1}:=(2;|v,|;2^{k}x).

{claim*}

For all
t\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, we have
{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}(c_{t})=c_{t+1}
and
\|c_{t}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}^{1}\leq 2^{k}|x|
.

Proof.

Let
t\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
. In configuration
c_{t}
, the machine is in State 1 and reads
b=1
on tape 1, so after one step, the machine is in configuration
c_{t+1}
. Moreover, we have
\|c_{t}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}^{1}\leq 2^{t+1}|x|\leq 2^{k}|x|
.
Now, let
t=k
. In configuration
c_{k}
, the machine is in State 1 and reads
b=0
on tape 1, so after one step, the machine is in configuration
c_{k+1}
. Moreover, we have
\|c_{k}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}^{1}\leq 2^{k}|x|
. This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Now, since
c_{0}=c
and
c_{k+1}=c^{\prime}
, the claim implies that
{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}^{k+1}(c)=c^{\prime},
and, moreover,

\|c\|_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}^{k+1}=\max_{t=0,\ldots,k}\|c_{t}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}^{1}\leq 2^{k}|x|.

(139)

This concludes the proof.
∎

We now design a TMNU that multiplies a real number
x\in\R
by some dyadic number
a\in[0,1)
, by reading some finite binary sequence
u
on the tape that encodes
a
in binary form. We define
\delta_{[0,1)}:\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to[0,1]
by

\delta_{[0,1)}(u)=\sum_{i=1}^{\len u}u_{i}2^{-i},\quad u\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}

(140)

We denote by
\Db_{1}^{+}
the output set of the function
\delta_{[0,1)}
, i.e.,

\Db_{1}^{+}:=\left\{\delta_{[0,1)}(u):u\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}\right\}\subseteq[0,1].

(141)

Note that
\Db_{1}^{+}
is dense in
[0,1]
, because for every
x\in[0,1]
and every
n\in
, there exists
u\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
|x-\delta_{[0,1)}(u)|\leq 2^{-n}
. We also define kind of inverse encoding by

u^{(a)}=\arg\min\{|u|:u\in\{0,1\}^{\ast},\delta_{[0,1)}(u)=a\},

(142)

for every
a\in\Db_{1}^{+}
.

Continuing with the philosophy introduced above, the TMNU we design to multiply
x
by some
a\in\Db_{1}^{+}
needs to be able to identify some prefix of an infinite binary sequence
u
written on the tape as encoding the number
a
, and to delete this prefix from the tape while multiplying
x
by
a
. In order to nonambiguously identify some prefix of
u
as encoding
a
, we will use the following prefix-free encoding of binary sequences, that we denote by
\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\cdot\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu:\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.
We first specify the prefix code used to delimit finite dyadic instructions.

{definition}
(Prefix-free encoding)
For
u\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, we let
\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\{0,1\}^{2\len u+1}
be defined as

\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2k}=u_{k},\quad\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2k-1}=1,\ \forall k\in\{1,\ldots,\len u\},

and
\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2\len u+1}=0
.

The TMNU we design has a neural state in dimension 2. Initially, the neural state contains
(x,0)
for some
x\in\R
, and the tape content is of the form
\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0muv
for some
u\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
encoding a number
a\in\Db_{1}^{+}
and some
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
. Then, the TMNU will exploit the prefix-free encoding to identify the prefix
\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
of the tape content as encoding the number
a
. Now, remark that
ax
can be rewritten as

ax=\sum_{k=1}^{\len u}u_{k}2^{-k}x=\sum_{k=1}^{\len u}u_{k}y_{k},

(143)

where
y_{k}:=2^{-k}x
for every
k\in\{1,\ldots,\len u\}
. Note that, in particular, we have
y_{k+1}=y_{k}/2
, for every
k\in\{1,\ldots,\len u-1\}
. Therefore, the TMNU will compute successively each term
y_{k}
on the first coordinate of the neural state, and will add
y_{k}
to the second coordinate of the neural state if
u_{k}=1
. This way, after reading the whole prefix
\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, the first coordinate of the neural state will contain
y_{\len u}=2^{-\len u}x
, and the second coordinate of the neural state will contain
\sum_{k=1}^{\len u}u_{k}y_{k}=ax
. Then, the TMNU will delete the prefix
\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
from the tape, and will halt with the first coordinate of the neural state containing
2^{-\len u}x
and the second coordinate of the neural state containing
ax
. The precise definition of this TMNU is given as follows.

{definition}

We let
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}
be the TMNU with neural dimension
2
and 3 states, defined by the following procedure.

•

State 1.
Let
b\in\{\square,0,1\}
be the symbol under scan.

i.

If
b\in\{0,\square\}
, update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow(\omega_{2},0),

and go to State 3.

ii.

If
b=1
, write
\square
, move right, update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow\left(\frac{1}{2}\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\right),

and go to State 2.

•

State 2.
Let
b\in\{\square,0,1\}
be the symbol under scan. Then, write
\square
, move right, update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow\left(\omega_{1},\omega_{2}+b\omega_{1}\right),

and go to State 1.

•

State 3.
Halt.

We now cast the discussion above into the following formal Lemma, that establishes that
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}
can be used to multiply a real number
x\in\R
by some dyadic number
a\in\Db_{1}^{+}
, by reading some finite binary sequence
u
on the tape that encodes
a
in binary form, and deleting this prefix from the tape.
The estimate also records that the auxiliary accumulation never exceeds the input magnitude.

{lemma}

Let
a\in\Db_{1}^{+},u:=u^{(a)},v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
, and
x\in\R
. Define the configurations
c:=(1;|\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0muv;x,0)
and
c^{\prime}:=(3;|v;ax,0)
. Then,

({\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+})^{\len{\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}}(c)=c^{\prime},\quad\|c\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}^{\len{\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}}\leq|x|.

(144)

Proof.

Let
a\in\Db_{1}^{+}
,
u:=u^{(a)}
,
n:=\len{u}
,
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
, and
x\in\R
. We define a sequence
(c_{t})_{0\leq t\leq 2n+1}
of configurations of
{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}
by

\begin{cases}c_{2t}&=\left(1;|\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2t+1:2n+1}v;2^{-t}x,a_{t}x\right),\quad\text{for all }t=0,\ldots,n,\\
c_{2t+1}&=\left(2;|\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2(t+1):2n+1}v;2^{-(t+1)}x,a_{t}x\right),\quad\text{for all }t=0,\ldots,n-1,\\
c_{2n+1}&=(3;|v;ax,0),\end{cases}

(145)

where
a_{t}:=\delta_{[0,1)}(u_{1:t})
for every
t\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
.

{claim*}

For all
t=0,\ldots,2n
, we have
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}(c_{t})=c_{t+1}
and
\|c_{t}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}^{1}\leq|x|
.

Proof of the Claim.

We divide the proof of the claim into three cases.

1.

Let
0\leq t\leq n-1
, and assume that the machine is in configuration
c_{2t}
. We have

c_{2t}=\left(1;|\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2t+1:2n+1}v;2^{-t}x,a_{t}x\right),

therefore the machine is in State 1 and reads
b:=\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2t+1}=1
on the tape. By Definition of State 1 of
{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}
(Definition
C.2
), the machine writes
\square
on the tape, moves the head one cell to the right, updates the neural state to
(\omega_{1}/2,\omega_{2})
, and goes to State 2. Hence, after one step, the machine is in configuration

\left(2;|\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2t+2:2n+1}v;2^{-(t+1)}x,a_{t}x\right)=c_{2t+1}.

Moreover, we have
\|c_{2t}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}^{1}\leq|x|
.

2.

Let
0\leq t\leq n-1
. Assume that the machine is in configuration
c_{2t+1}
. We have

c_{2t+1}=\left(2;|\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2(t+1):2n+1}v;2^{-(t+1)}x,a_{t}x\right),

therefore the machine is in State 2 and reads
b:=\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2(t+1)}=u_{t+1}
on the tape. By Definition of State 2 of
{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}
(Definition
C.2
), the machine writes
\square
on the tape, moves the head one cell to the right, updates the neural state to
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2}+b\cdot\omega_{1})
, where

\omega_{2}+b\cdot\omega_{1}=a_{t}x+u_{t+1}\cdot 2^{-(t+1)}x=a_{t+1}x,

and goes to State 1. Hence, after one step, the machine is in configuration

\left(1;|\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2t+3:2n+1}v;2^{-(t+1)}x,a_{t+1}x\right)=c_{2t+2}.

Moreover, we have
\|c_{2t+1}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}^{1}\leq|x|
.

3.

Case
t=2n
. Assume that the machine is in configuration

c_{2n}:=\left(1;|\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2n+1:2n+1}v;2^{-n}x,a_{n}x\right)=\left(1;|\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2n+1}v;2^{-n}x,ax\right),

so the machine is in State 1, and reads the symbol
b=\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2n+1}=0
. By Definition of State 1 of
{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}
(Definition
C.2
), the machine updates the neural state to
\left(ax,0\right)
, and goes to State 3. This implies that, after one time step, the machine is in configuration
(3;|v;ax,0)=c_{2n+1}
. Moreover, we have
\|c_{2n}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}^{1}\leq|x|
.

This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Now, since
c=c_{0}
and
c^{\prime}=c_{2n+1}
, the claim implies that

({\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+})^{2n+1}(c)=c^{\prime},

(146)

and, moreover,

\|c\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}^{2n+1}=\max_{0\leq t\leq 2n}\|c_{t}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}^{1}\leq|x|.

(147)

Since
\len{\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}=2n+1
, this concludes the proof.
∎

As a final example of the philosophy of building TMNUs that identify a finite prefix of an infinite sequence written on their tape as an instruction to be executed, we define a family of TMNUs that simulate RNNs. Specifically, for every RNN
\Rc
, we define a TMNU
{\bf M}_{\Rc}
such that given that
1^{n}0u
is initially written on the tape and the neural state contains some
x\in\R^{d}
(where
d
is the input dimension of
\Rc
), the TMNU erases
1^{n}0
from its tape, and produces
\Rc\Dc x[n]
on its neural state. This allows us, in particular, to import any results already present in the literature for RNNs to TMNUs. In particular, this allows to show that TMNUs can approximate the product of two real numbers, by simulating the RNN defined in
[
14
, Theorem 11]
. We define formally the TMNU that simulates an RNN.

{definition}

Let
\Rc:=(d,m,d^{\prime};A_{h},b_{h},A_{x},A_{o},b_{o})
be an RNN. We define the TMNU
{\bf M}_{\Rc}
with neural dimension
d:=m
and 4 states, defined by the following procedure.

•

State 1.
Update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow A_{x}\omega_{1:d}+b_{h},

and go to State 2.

•

State 2.
Update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow\rho(\omega)

and go to State 3.

•

State 3.
Let
b\in\{\square,0,1\}
be the symbol under scan.

i.

If
b\in\{0,\square\}
, write
\square
, move right, update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow\begin{bmatrix}A_{o}\\
0\end{bmatrix}\omega+\begin{bmatrix}b_{o}\\
0\end{bmatrix},

and go to State 4.

ii.

If
b=1
, write
\square
, move right, update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow A_{h}\omega+b_{h}

and go to State 2.

•

State 4.
Halt.

The simulation property of this machine is recorded next.

{lemma}

Let
\Rc:=(d,m,d^{\prime};A_{h},b_{h},A_{x},A_{o},b_{o})
be an RNN, and let
{\bf M}_{\Rc}
be the TMNU defined in Definition
C.2
. Let
n\in
,
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
, and
x\in\R^{d}
. Define the configurations
c:=(1;|1^{n}0v;x,0,\ldots,0)
and
c^{\prime}:=(4;|v;\Rc\Dc x[n],0,\ldots,0)
. Then,

{\bf M}_{\Rc}^{2n+3}(c)=c^{\prime},

(148)

and

\|c\|_{{\bf M}_{\Rc}}^{2n+3}\leq\|x\|_{\infty}\vee\|A_{x}x+b_{h}\|_{\infty}\vee\|\Rc\Dc x[n]\|_{\infty}\vee\max_{0\leq t\leq n}\|\Hc\Dc x[t]\|_{\infty}\vee\max_{0\leq t\leq n}\|A_{h}\Hc\Dc x[t]+b_{h}\|_{\infty},

(149)

where
\Hc
is the hidden state operator of
\Rc
as defined in Definition
2
.

Proof.

Let
\Rc:=(d,m,d^{\prime};A_{h},b_{h},A_{x},A_{o},b_{o})
be an RNN,
n\in\No
,
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
, and
x\in\R^{d}
. We define a sequence of configurations
(c_{t})_{0\leq t\leq 2n+3}
of
{\bf M}_{\Rc}
by

\begin{cases}c_{0}&=(1;|1^{n}0v;x,0,\ldots,0),\\
c_{1}&=(2;|1^{n}0v;A_{x}x+b_{h}),\\
c_{2t}&=(3;|1^{n-t+1}0v;\Hc\Dc(x)[t-1]),\quad\text{for all }t=1,\ldots,n+1,\\
c_{2t+1}&=(2;|1^{n-t}0v;A_{h}\Hc\Dc(x)[t-1]+b_{h}),\quad\text{for all }t=1,\ldots,n,\\
c_{2n+3}&=(4;|v;\Rc\Dc(x)[n],0,\ldots,0).\end{cases}

(150)

We also let

B:=\|x\|_{\infty}\vee\|A_{x}x+b_{h}\|_{\infty}\vee\|\Rc\Dc x[n]\|_{\infty}\vee\max_{0\leq t\leq n}\|\Hc\Dc x[t]\|_{\infty}\vee\max_{0\leq t\leq n}\|A_{h}\Hc\Dc x[t]+b_{h}\|_{\infty}.

{claim*}

For every
t\in\{0,\ldots,2n+2\}
, we have
{\bf M}_{\Rc}(c_{t})=c_{t+1}
.

Proof of the claim.

First note that
{\bf M}_{\Rc}(c_{0})=c_{1}
by State 1 of
{\bf M}_{\Rc}
(Definition
C.2
), and that
{\bf M}_{\Rc}(c_{1})=c_{2}
by State 2 of
{\bf M}_{\Rc}
. Moreover, in configuration
c_{2n+2}=(3;|0v;\Hc\Dc x[n])
, the machine is in State 3 and reads a symbol
0
on its tape. Therefore,
{\bf M}_{\Rc}(c_{2n+2})=c_{2n+3}
.

Now, let
t\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. We have
c_{2t}=(3;|1^{n-t+1}0v;\Hc\Dc x[t-1])
, so the machine is in State 3 and reads the symbol
b=1
on the tape. By State 3 of
{\bf M}_{\Rc}
, we have
{\bf M}_{\Rc}(c_{2t})=c_{2t+1}
, since
c_{2t+1}=(2;|1^{n-t}0v;A_{h}\Hc\Dc x[t-1]+b_{h})
. Moreover, we have
c_{2t+1}=(2;|1^{n-t}0v;A_{h}\Hc\Dc x[t-1]+b_{h})
, so the machine is in State 2, and by State 2 of
{\bf M}_{\Rc}
, we have
{\bf M}_{\Rc}(c_{2t+1})=c_{2(t+1)}
, since
c_{2(t+1)}=(3;|1^{n-t}0v;\Hc\Dc x[t])
. This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Since
c=c_{0}
and
c^{\prime}=c_{2n+3}
, the claim implies that

{\bf M}_{\Rc}^{2n+3}(c)=c^{\prime}.

(151)

Moreover, by denoting
\omega_{t}
the neural state of configuration
c_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle\|c\|_{{\bf M}_{\Rc}}^{2n+3}=\max_{0\leq t\leq 2n+2}\|c_{t}\|_{{\bf M}_{\Rc}}^{1}\leq\max_{0\leq t\leq 2n+3}\|\omega_{t}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\max\{\|x\|_{\infty},\|A_{x}x+b_{h}\|_{\infty},\|\Rc\Dc x[n]\|_{\infty},\max_{0\leq t\leq n}\|\Hc\Dc x[t]\|_{\infty},\max_{0\leq t\leq n}\|A_{h}\Hc\Dc x[t]+b_{h}\|_{\infty}\}

\displaystyle=B,

by definition of
B
. This concludes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Now, we can use the following result from the literature on RNNs to design a TMNU that can approximate the multiplication of two inputs
x
and
y
.

{lemma}
[
14
, Theorem 11]

There exists an RNN
\Rc^{\times}:=(2,14,1;A_{h},b_{h},A_{x},A_{o},b_{o})
, such that for every
x\in[-1,1]^{2}
,

\left|\Rc^{\times}\Dc x[n]-(x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\right|\leq 2^{-2n-1},\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|\Hc^{\times}\Dc x[n]\right\|_{\infty},\left\|A_{h}\Hc^{\times}\Dc x[n]+b_{h}\right\|_{\infty},\left|\Rc^{\times}\Dc x[n]\right|\leq 1,

for every
n\in
, and
A_{x}x+b_{h}\in[-1,1]^{14}
.

We turn this RNN primitive into a TMNU by applying the simulation construction above.

{definition}

We let
{\boldsymbol{\times}}
be the TMNU defined as the TMNU
{\bf M}_{\Rc^{\times}}
of Definition
C.2
, where
\Rc^{\times}
is the RNN of Lemma
C.2
.

The resulting machine inherits the approximation and boundedness properties of the RNN multiplier.

{lemma}

Let
n\in\No
,
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
, and
x,y\in[-1,1]
. Define the configurations
c:=(1;|1^{n}0v;x,y,0_{12})
and
c^{\prime}:=(4;|v;\Rc^{\times}\Dc(x,y)[n],0_{13})
. Then,

{\boldsymbol{\times}}^{2n+3}(c)=c^{\prime},\quad\|c\|_{\boldsymbol{\times}}^{2n+3}\leq 1.

(152)

Proof.

Let
\Rc^{\times}:=(2,14,1;A_{h},b_{h},A_{x},A_{o},b_{o})
be the RNN of Lemma
C.2
, and let
\Hc^{\times}
be its hidden state operator. By Lemma
C.2
, we have
{\boldsymbol{\times}}^{2n+3}(c)=c^{\prime}
and

\displaystyle\|c\|_{\boldsymbol{\times}}^{2n+3}

\displaystyle\leq|x|\vee|y|\vee\|A_{x}(x,y)+b_{h}\|_{\infty}\vee|\Rc^{\times}\Dc(x,y)[n]|

\displaystyle\quad\vee\max_{0\leq t\leq n}\|\Hc^{\times}\Dc(x,y)[t]\|_{\infty}\vee\max_{0\leq t\leq n}\|A_{h}\Hc^{\times}\Dc(x,y)[t]+b_{h}\|_{\infty}.

Since
(x,y)\in[-1,1]^{2}
, we have
|x|\vee|y|\leq 1
. Moreover, Lemma
C.2
implies that
\|A_{x}(x,y)+b_{h}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
,
|\Rc^{\times}\Dc(x,y)[n]|\leq 1
, and, by applying the same lemma for each
t\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
,

\max_{0\leq t\leq n}\|\Hc^{\times}\Dc(x,y)[t]\|_{\infty}\vee\max_{0\leq t\leq n}\|A_{h}\Hc^{\times}\Dc(x,y)[t]+b_{h}\|_{\infty}\leq 1.

This concludes the proof.
∎

We now have defined four TMNUs that allows to perform some simple operations: multiplication by
-1
, multiplication by
2^{k}
for some
k\in_{0}
, multiplication by some
a\in\Db_{1}^{+}
, and approximation of the multiplication of two inputs. These TMNUs can be combined to design more complex TMNUs that perform more complex operations. Specifically, by combining these operations we can design a TMNU that approximate any polynomial, and consequently, and any continuous function. In the next section, we introduce the notion of subroutine for TMNUs, that will allow us to design more complex TMNUs by combining simpler TMNUs as building blocks.

Now equipped with this tool for combining TMNUs, we are ready to design more complex TMNUs that can perform more complex operations, by combining simpler TMNUs as building blocks. As a first example, we can combine the TMNU
{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}
and the TMNU
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}
to design a TMNU that can multiply its input by some
a\in\Db_{1}:=\Db_{1}^{+}\cup\left(-\Db_{1}^{+}\right)
. For every
a\in\Db_{1}
, we let

s^{(a)}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }a\geq 0,\\
1,&\text{if }a<0,\end{cases}

and define
v^{(a)}:=s^{(a)}\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu^{(|a|)}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
. We now introduce the TMNU
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}
that can multiply its input by some
a\in\Db_{1}
by reading some finite binary sequence
u
on the tape that encodes
a
in binary form, and deleting this prefix from the tape.

{definition}

We let
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}
be the TMNU with neural dimension
2
and 4 states, defined by the following procedure.

•

State 1.
Use
{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}
as a subroutine with correspondence
(1,2;1)
.

•

State 2-3.
Use
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}
as a subroutine with correspondence
(2,3,4;1,2)
.

•

State 4.
Halt.

By using the Subroutine Lemma
C.1
, we can easily verify that
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}
can be used to multiply an input
x
by some
a\in\Db_{1}
, by reading some finite binary sequence
u
on the tape that encodes
a
in binary form, and deleting this prefix from the tape, as follows.

{lemma}

Let
a\in\Db_{1},u:=v^{(a)},v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
, and
x\in\R
. Define the configurations
c:=(1;|uv;x,0)
and
c^{\prime}:=(4;|v;ax,0)
. Then,

{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{\len u}(c)=c^{\prime},\quad\|c\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}}^{\len u}\leq|x|.

(153)

Proof.

Let
a\in\Db_{1}
,
u:=v^{(a)}
,
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
, and
x\in\R
. Define the configurations

c_{0}:=(1;|uv;x,0),\quad c_{1}:=(2;|u_{2:\len u}v;(-1)^{u_{1}}x,0),\quad c_{2}:=(4;|v;ax,0).

We first show that
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}(c_{0})=c_{1}
and
\|c_{0}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{1}\leq|x|
. By definition of State 1 of
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}
(Definition
C.2
), the machine uses the TMNU
{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}
as a subroutine with correspondence
(1,2;1)
. Let

c_{0}^{\prime}:=(1;|uv;x),\quad c_{1}^{\prime}:=(2;|u_{2:\len u}v;(-1)^{u_{1}}x)

be configurations of
{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}
. Then,

\operatorname{Sh}_{(1,2),(1)}(c_{0})=c_{0}^{\prime},\quad\operatorname{Lift}_{(1,2),(1)}^{0}(c_{1}^{\prime})=c_{1}.

By Lemma
C.2
, we have
{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}(c_{0}^{\prime})=c_{1}^{\prime}
and
\|c_{0}^{\prime}\|_{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}^{1}\leq|x|
. Therefore, by Lemma
C.1
, we obtain

{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}(c_{0})=\operatorname{Lift}_{(1,2),(1)}^{0}({\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}(c_{0}^{\prime}))=c_{1}

and

\|c_{0}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{1}\leq\|c_{0}^{\prime}\|_{\boldsymbol{{\pm}}}^{1}\vee\|c_{0}\|\leq|x|.

We now show that
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{\len u-1}(c_{1})=c_{2}
and
\|c_{1}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{\len u-1}\leq|x|
. By definition of States 2 and 3 of
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}
(Definition
C.2
), the machine uses the TMNU
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}
as a subroutine with correspondence
(2,3,4;1,2)
. Let

\tilde{c}_{1}:=(1;|u_{2:\len u}v;(-1)^{u_{1}}x,0),\quad\tilde{c}_{2}:=(3;|v;|a|(-1)^{u_{1}}x,0)=(3;|v;ax,0)

be configurations of
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}
. Then,

\operatorname{Sh}_{(2,3,4),(1,2)}(c_{1})=\tilde{c}_{1},\quad\operatorname{Lift}_{(2,3,4),(1,2)}^{\varnothing}(\tilde{c}_{2})=c_{2}.

Since
u_{2:\len u}=\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0muu^{(|a|)}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
and
\len{u_{2:\len u}}=\len u-1
, Lemma
C.2
gives

({\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+})^{\len u-1}(\tilde{c}_{1})=\tilde{c}_{2},\quad\|\tilde{c}_{1}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}^{\len u-1}\leq|x|.

Therefore, by Lemma
C.1
, we obtain

{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{\len u-1}(c_{1})=\operatorname{Lift}_{(2,3,4),(1,2)}^{\varnothing}\left(({\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+})^{\len u-1}(\tilde{c}_{1})\right)=c_{2}

and

\|c_{1}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{\len u-1}\leq\|\tilde{c}_{1}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{+}}^{\len u-1}\vee\|c_{1}\|\leq|x|.

Combining the two stages, and using
c=c_{0}
and
c^{\prime}=c_{2}
, we obtain

{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{\len u}(c)=c^{\prime},

and

\|c\|_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{\len u}=\|c_{0}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{1}\vee\|c_{1}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{\len u-1}\leq|x|.

∎

In the incoming section, we use the Subroutine Lemma
C.1
in combination with the elementary TMNUs defined in previous section in order to build a TMNU that approximate any polynomial with dyadic coefficients.

C.3
Approximation of polynomials with dyadic coefficients

In this section, we show how to use the previously defined TMNUs in order to approximate polynomials with dyadic coefficients. Specifically, we will design a TMNU that realises the following approximation of a polynomial
P=a_{0}+a_{1}x+\ldots+a_{d}x^{d}
, with
d\in\No
and
a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}\in\Db:=\Z+\Db_{1}
. For
d,n\in\No
, we define the function
G_{d,n}:[-1,1]\to[-1,1]
by

G_{0,n}(x)=1,\quad G_{d+1,n}(x)=\Rc^{\times}\Dc(G_{d,n}(x),x)[n],\quad d\in,n\in\No,x\in[-1,1],

and further, for
n\in\No
, the function
F_{P,n}:[-1,1]\to\R
by

F_{P,n}(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}a_{i}G_{i,n}(x).

The function
F_{P,n}
is precisely the function that we will realize with a TMNU, by computing succesively
G_{0,n}(x),G_{1,n}(x),\ldots,G_{d,n}(x)
, and then combining these values with linear transformations to obtain
F_{P,n}(x)
. In Appendix
D
are shown the following bounds on the magnitude and on the approximation rate of
G_{d,n}
and
F_{P,n}
, for
n,d\in\No
. In this Lemma, we use the quantities

\|P\|_{0}:=d+1,\quad\text{and}\quad\|P\|_{1}:=|a_{0}|+|a_{1}|+\cdots+|a_{d}|,

(154)

where
P=a_{0}+a_{1}x+\ldots+a_{d}x^{d}
is a polynomial with
d\in\No
and
a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}\in\R
.

{lemma}

Let
d,n\in\No
and
P
be a polynomial. Then, for all
x\in[-1,1]
, we have
|G_{d,n}(x)|\leq 1
,
|F_{P,n}(x)|\leq\|P\|_{1}
,

\left|G_{d,n}(x)-x^{d}\right|\leq d2^{-2n-1},\quad\text{and}\quad|F_{P,n}(x)-P(x)|\leq\|P\|_{1}\|P\|_{0}2^{-2n-1}.

We now explain how we design a TMNU that computes
F_{P,n}
. The idea is that the TMNU starts with its tape containing an encoding of the coefficients
a_{0},\ldots a_{d}\in\Db
of
P
, and an encoding of
n\in\No
, and with its neural state containing
(x,1,0)=(x,G_{0,n}(x),0)
for some
x\in[-1,1]
, that is transformed successively into
(x,G_{1,n}(x),a_{0}G_{0,n}(x))
,
(x,G_{2,n}(x),a_{0}G_{0,n}(x)+a_{1}G_{1,n}(x))
, …,
(x,G_{d,n}(x),a_{0}G_{0,n}(x)+a_{1}G_{1,n}(x)+\cdots+a_{d-1}G_{d-1,n}(x))
, and finally into
(x,G_{d,n}(x),F_{P,n}(x))
. We will specifically design a TMNU
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
that implements one step of this procedure, that is, that transforms
(x,G_{i,n}(x),\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{j}G_{j,n}(x))
into
(x,G_{i+1,n}(x),\sum_{j=0}^{i}a_{j}G_{j,n}(x))
, for any
i\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
. Then, we will use
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
as a subroutine of a larger TMNU that implements the whole procedure.

Before proceeding, we want to add a small correction to the preceding description of the procedure. In view of the simulation of the TMNU we build by an RNN, we need to make sure that the neural state of the TMNU remains bounded below some constant, as explained at the beginning of Section
6
. In the procedure above, the neural state is bounded by
\|P\|_{1}
, so this TMNU can indeed be simulated by an RNN. However, ultimately, this TMNU will be used as a subroutine of a larger TMNU that approximates continuous functions by means of a sequence
(P_{i})_{i\in\No}
of approximating polynomials, that may satisfy
\|P_{i}\|_{1}\underset{i\to\infty}{\to}\infty
. To cope with this issue, we will make a slight modification of the procedure above, that guarantees that the neural state of the TMNU remains bounded by
\|P\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}+\|P\|_{0}\|P\|_{1}2^{-2n-1}
. This way, when approximating a sequence of polynomials
(P_{i})_{i\in\No}
that converges uniformly to some continuous function, we can always choose
n
large enough so that
\|P_{i}\|_{0}\|P_{i}\|_{1}2^{-2n-1}\leq 1
, and moreover, the uniform convergence of
(P_{i})_{i\in\No}
implies that there exists some constant
M>0
such that
\|P_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}\leq M
for all
i\in\No
, hence the neural state of the TMNU remains bounded by
M+1
when approximating the sequence
(P_{i})_{i\in\No}
, and can therefore be simulated by an RNN. We now describe how to modify the above procedure. For every polynomial
P\neq 0
, define
k_{P}:=1+\lfloor\log_{2}\|P\|_{1}\rfloor\vee 0
, and
P^{*}:=2^{-k_{P}}P
. Note that
\|P^{\ast}\|_{\infty}\leq\|P^{*}\|_{1}<1
. Then, instead of computing
F_{P,n}(x)
, we will compute
F_{P^{*},n}(x)
, and then multiply the result by
2^{k_{P}}
to obtain
F_{P,n}(x)
. Therefore, during the process of computing
F_{P^{*},n}(x)
, the neural state of the TMNU remains bounded by
\|P^{*}\|_{1}<1
, and at the end of the process, the TMNU multiplies the result by
2^{k_{P}}
to obtain
F_{P,n}(x)
, so the neural state remains bounded below
|2^{k_{P}}F_{P^{\ast},n}(x)|=|F_{P,n}(x)|\leq\|P\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}+\|P\|_{0}\|P\|_{1}2^{-2n-1}
, by Lemma
C.3
.

We now give the formal definition of the TMNU
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
described above.
This machine performs one coefficient update and one monomial update.

{definition}

We define the TMNU
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
with neural dimension
16
and 9 states, defined by the following procedure.

•

State 1.
Update the neural state as
\omega\leftarrow(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3},\omega_{2},0_{12}),

and go to State 2.

•

State 2-4.
Use
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}
(Def.
C.2
) as subroutine with correspondence
(2,3,4,5;4,5)
.

•

State 5.
Update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3}+\omega_{4},\omega_{1},0_{12}),

and go to State 6.

•

State 6-8.
Use
{\boldsymbol{\times}}
(Def.
C.2
) as subroutine with correspondence
(6,7,8,9;2,4,\ldots,16)
.

•

State 9.
Halt.

We now show that
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
, in essence, implements one step of the procedure described above, that is, it transforms
(x,G_{i,n}(x),\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{j}G_{j,n}(x))
into
(x,G_{i+1,n}(x),\sum_{j=0}^{i}a_{j}G_{j,n}(x))
, for any
i\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
, by reading some finite binary sequence
u
on the tape that encodes
a_{i}
in binary form, and deleting this prefix from the tape, and by reading some finite binary sequence
1^{n}0
on the tape that encodes
n
, and deleting this prefix from the tape. In fact, what we prove is slightly more general, as we show that
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
transforms
(x,G_{i,n}(x),z)
into
(x,G_{i+1,n}(x),z+a_{i}G_{i,n}(x))
, for any
x\in[-1,1]
,
z\in\R
,
i,n\in\No
.

{lemma}

Let
n,i\in\No
,
a\in\Db_{1}
,
u:=v^{(a)}
, and
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
,
x\in[-1,1]
, and
z\in\R
. Define
T:=\len{u}+2n+5
and the configurations
c:=(1;|u1^{n}0v;x,G_{i,n}(x),z,0_{13})
and
c^{\prime}:=(9;|v;x,G_{i+1,n}(x),z+aG_{i,n}(x),0_{13})
. Then,

\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}^{T}(c)=c^{\prime},\quad\|c\|_{\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}}^{T}\leq 1\vee(|z|+|a|).

(155)

Proof.

Let
n,i\in\No
,
a\in\Db_{1}
,
u:=v^{(a)}
,
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
,
x\in[-1,1]
,
z\in\R
, and define
y:=G_{i,n}(x)
. We set

T_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}:=\len u,\quad T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}:=2n+3,\quad\text{and}\quad T:=T_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}+T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}+2=\len u+2n+5.

We define the configurations
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}
of
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
by

\begin{cases}c_{0}&=(1;|u1^{n}0v;x,y,z,0_{13}),\\
c_{1}&=(2;|u1^{n}0v;x,y,z,y,0_{12}),\\
c_{2}&=(5;|1^{n}0v;x,y,z,ay,0_{12}),\\
c_{3}&=(6;|1^{n}0v;x,y,z+ay,x,0_{12}),\\
c_{4}&=(9;|v;x,G_{i+1,n}(x),z+ay,0_{13}).\end{cases}

(156)

{claim*}

The following four statements hold.

(a)

\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}(c_{0})=c_{1}
and
\|c_{0}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}}^{1}\leq 1\vee|z|
.

(b)

\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}}(c_{1})=c_{2}
and
\|c_{1}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}}\leq 1\vee|z|
.

(c)

\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}(c_{2})=c_{3}
and
\|c_{2}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}}^{1}\leq 1\vee(|z|+|a|)
.

(d)

\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}(c_{3})=c_{4}
and
\|c_{3}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}\leq 1\vee(|z|+|a|)
.

Proof.

We prove each statement in turn.

(a)

Assume that the machine is in configuration
c_{0}
. The machine is in State 1, so after one step, the machine is in configuration
c_{1}
. Moreover, we have

\|c_{0}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}}^{1}\leq\|(x,y,z,y,0_{12})\|_{\infty}\leq 1\vee|z|,

where we used
|x|\leq 1
and
|y|=|G_{i,n}(x)|\leq 1
by Lemma
C.3
.

(b)

Let
c_{1}^{\prime}:=(1;|u1^{n}0v;y,0)
, and
c^{\prime}_{2}:=(4;|1^{n}0v;ay,0)
. Then,

\operatorname{Sh}_{(2,3,4,5),(4,5)}(c_{1})=c_{1}^{\prime},\quad\operatorname{Lift}_{(2,3,4,5),(4,5)}^{(x,y,z,0_{11})}(c_{2}^{\prime})=c_{2}.

By Lemma
C.2
, we have
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}}(c_{1}^{\prime})=c^{\prime}_{2}
and
\|c_{1}^{\prime}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}}\leq|y|\leq 1
. Since
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}
is a subroutine of
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
with correspondance
(2,3,4,5;4,5)
by Definition
C.3
, Lemma
C.1
yields

\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}}(c_{1})=\operatorname{Lift}_{(2,3,4,5),(4,5)}^{(x,y,z,0_{11})}\left({\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}}(c_{1}^{\prime})\right)=c_{2},

and

\|c_{1}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}}\leq\|c_{1}^{\prime}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}}\vee\|c_{1}\|\leq 1\vee|z|.

(c)

Assume that the machine is in configuration
c_{2}
. The machine is in State 5, so after one step, the machine is in configuration
c_{3}
. Moreover, we have

\|c_{2}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}}^{1}\leq\|(x,y,z,ay,0_{12})\|_{\infty}\vee\|(x,y,z+ay,x,0_{12})\|_{\infty}\leq 1\vee(|z|+|a|).

(d)

Let
c_{3}^{\prime}:=(1;|1^{n}0v;y,x,0_{12})
, and
c^{\prime}_{4}:=(4;|v;\Rc^{\times}\Dc(y,x)[n],0_{13})=(4;|v;G_{i+1,n}(x),0_{13})
. Then,

\operatorname{Sh}_{(6,7,8,9),(2,4,\ldots,16)}(c_{3})=c_{3}^{\prime},\quad\operatorname{Lift}_{(6,7,8,9),(2,4,\ldots,16)}^{(x,z+ay)}(c_{4}^{\prime})=c_{4}.

By Lemma
C.2
, we have
{\boldsymbol{\times}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}(c_{3}^{\prime})=c^{\prime}_{4}
and
\|c_{3}^{\prime}\|_{\boldsymbol{\times}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}\leq 1
. Since
{\boldsymbol{\times}}
is a subroutine of
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
with correspondence
(6,7,8,9;2,4,\ldots,16)
by Definition
C.3
, Lemma
C.1
yields

\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}(c_{3})=\operatorname{Lift}_{(6,7,8,9),(2,4,\ldots,16)}^{(x,z+ay)}\left({\boldsymbol{\times}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}(c_{3}^{\prime})\right)=c_{4},

and

\|c_{3}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}\leq\|c_{3}^{\prime}\|_{\boldsymbol{\times}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}\vee\|c_{3}\|\overset{(a)}{\leq}1\vee(|z|+|a|),

where (a) is by
|x|\leq 1
,
|G_{i+1,n}(x)|\leq 1
and
|y|=|G_{i,n}(x)|\leq 1
, where
|G_{j,n}(x)|\leq 1
for
j=i,i+1
by Lemma
C.3
.

This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Now, by invoking repeatedly the Chaining Lemma (Lemma
C
) together with the claim, and using
c=c_{0}
and
c^{\prime}=c_{4}
, we obtain

\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}^{T}(c)=c^{\prime},

and

\|c\|_{\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}}^{T}\leq 1\vee(|z|+|a|).

This concludes the proof.
∎

We finally design a TMNU that uses the TMNU
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
as a subroutine to compute
F_{P,n}(x)
, for any polynomial
P
with dyadic coefficients, any
n\in\No
, and any
x\in[-1,1]
. In order to implement this TMNU in a way that its neural state remains bounded by
\|P\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}+\|P\|_{0}\|P\|_{1}2^{-2n-1}
, we will first compute
F_{P^{*},n}(x)
, where
P^{*}:=2^{-k_{P}}P
is the rescaled version of
P
defined above, and then we will multiply the result by
2^{k_{P}}
to obtain
F_{P,n}(x)
. This TMNU will parse an encoding of the coefficients of
P^{\ast}
, of
n
and
k_{P}
on its tape, that we formally define as follows.

{definition}

Let
P:x\mapsto a_{0}x^{0}+a_{1}x^{1}+\cdots+a_{d}x^{d}
be a polynomial with coefficients
a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}\in\Db
, and let
n\in\No
. We define

u_{P}:=v^{(a_{0}^{\ast})}\cdot 1\cdot v^{(a_{1}^{\ast})}\cdot 1\cdot v^{(a_{2}^{\ast})}\cdots 1\cdot v^{(a_{d}^{\ast})}\cdot 0\cdot 1^{k_{P}}0,

and

u_{P,n}:=v^{(a_{0}^{\ast})}1^{n}0\cdot 1\cdot v^{(a_{1}^{\ast})}1^{n}0\cdot 1\cdot v^{(a_{2}^{\ast})}1^{n}0\cdots 1\cdot v^{(a_{d}^{\ast})}1^{n}0\cdot 0\cdot 1^{k_{P}}0,

where
a_{0}^{\ast},\ldots,a_{d}^{\ast}\in\Db_{1}
are the coefficients of the polynomial
P^{\ast}
.

We now formally introduce the TMNU that computes
F_{P,n}(x)
, for any polynomial
P
with dyadic coefficients, any
n\in\No
, and any
x\in[-1,1]
. This machine iterates
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
across all coefficients and then rescales the result.

{definition}

We let
{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}
be the TMNU with neural dimension
16
and 11 states, defined by the following procedure.

•

State 1-8.
Use
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
(Def.
C.3
) as subroutine with correspondence
(1,\ldots,9;1,\ldots,16)
.

•

State 9.
Let
b\in\{\square,0,1\}
under scan.

i.

If
b\in\{0,\square\}
, write
\square
, move right, update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow(\omega_{1},\omega_{3},0_{14}),

and go to State 10.

ii.

If
b=1
, write
\square
, move right and go to State 1.

•

State 10.
Use
{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}
(Def.
C.2
) as subroutine with correspondence
(10,11;2)
.

•

State 11.
Halt.

The next lemma verifies the complete polynomial-evaluation cycle and its bound.

{lemma}

Let
n\in\No
,
P\in\Db[X]
,
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
, and
x\in[-1,1]
. Define the configurations

c:=\left(1;|u_{P,n}v;x,1,0_{14}\right)
,
and

c^{\prime}:=\left(11;|v;x,F_{P,n}(x),0_{14}\right)
.
Define
T:=\len{u_{P}}+\|P\|_{0}(2n+5)
. Then,

{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}^{T}(c)=c^{\prime},\quad\|c\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}}^{T}\leq 1\vee|F_{P,n}(x)|.

(157)

Proof.

Let
n\in\No
,
P
a polynomial of degree
d\in
with coefficients
a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}\in\Db
,
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
, and
x\in[-1,1]
. Let
b_{0},\ldots,b_{d}\in\Db_{1}
be the coefficients of
P^{\ast}
, and define the polynomials
P^{\ast}_{0}=0
, and
P^{\ast}_{1},\ldots,P^{\ast}_{d+1}
by

P^{\ast}_{i}:x\mapsto b_{0}x^{0}+b_{1}x^{1}+\cdots+b_{i-1}x^{i-1},\ \quad i\in\{1,\ldots,d+1\}.

Define also

u^{(i)}:=v^{(b_{i})}1^{n}0\cdot 1\cdot v^{(b_{i+1})}1^{n}0\cdots 1\cdot v^{(b_{d})}1^{n}0,\ \quad i\in\{0,\ldots,d\},

and
u^{(d+1)}=\epsilon
. Note that
u^{(0)}\cdot 0\cdot 1^{k_{P}}0=u_{P,n}
.
For
t\in\{0,\ldots,d\}
, set

T_{t}:=\len{v^{(b_{t})}}+2n+5,

and set
T_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}:=k_{P}+1
and
T:=\len{u_{P}}+\|P\|_{0}(2n+5)
.
We define the configurations
c_{t}
of
{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}
for
t\in\{0,\ldots,2d+3\}
by

\begin{cases}c_{2t}&:=\left(1;|u^{(t)}01^{k_{P}}0v,|;x,G_{t,n}(x),F_{P^{\ast}_{t},n}(x),0_{13}\right),\quad t\in\{0,\ldots,d\},\\
c_{2t+1}&:=\left(9;|1u^{(t+1)}01^{k_{P}}0v,|;x,G_{t+1,n}(x),F_{P^{\ast}_{t+1},n}(x),0_{13}\right),\quad t\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\},\\
c_{2d+1}&:=\left(9;|01^{k_{P}}0v,|;x,G_{d+1,n}(x),F_{P^{\ast},n}(x),0_{13}\right),\\
c_{2d+2}&:=\left(10;|1^{k_{P}}0v;x,F_{P^{\ast},n}(x),0_{14}\right),\\
c_{2d+3}&:=\left(11;|v;x,F_{P,n}(x),0_{14}\right).\end{cases}

(158)

{claim*}

The following four statements hold.

(a)

For all
t\in\{0,\ldots,d\}
,
{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}^{T_{t}}(c_{2t})=c_{2t+1}
and
\|c_{2t}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}}^{T_{t}}\leq 1
.

(b)

For all
t\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
,
{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}(c_{2t+1})=c_{2t+2}
and
\|c_{2t+1}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}}^{1}\leq 1
.

(c)

{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}(c_{2d+1})=c_{2d+2}
and
\|c_{2d+1}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}}^{1}\leq 1
.

(d)

{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}}(c_{2d+2})=c_{2d+3}
and
\|c_{2d+2}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}}\leq 1\vee|F_{P,n}(x)|
.

Proof of the Claim.

We prove each statement in turn.

(a)

Let
t\in\{0,\ldots,d\}
. Note that we can write
c_{2t}
and
c_{2t+1}
as

c_{2t}=(1;|v^{(b_{t})}1^{n}0\tilde{v};x,G_{t,n}(x),z,0_{13}),

and

c_{2t+1}=(9;|\tilde{v};x,G_{t+1,n}(x),z+b_{t}G_{t,n}(x),0_{13}),

where
z:=F_{P^{\ast}_{t},n}(x)
, and
\tilde{v}:=1u^{(t+1)}01^{k_{P}}0v
if
t<d
, and
\tilde{v}:=01^{k_{P}}0v
if
t=d
. Therefore, by Lemma
C.3
, we have
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}^{T_{t}}(c_{2t})=c_{2t+1}
and

\displaystyle\|c_{2t}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}}^{T_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq 1\vee(|z|+|b_{t}|)\overset{(a)}{\leq}1\vee(\|P^{\ast}_{t}\|_{1}+|b_{t}|)\leq 1\vee\|P^{\ast}_{t+1}\|_{1}\leq 1\vee\|P^{\ast}\|_{1}\leq 1,

(159)

where (a) follows by
|z|=|F_{P^{\ast}_{t},n}(x)|\leq\|P^{\ast}_{t}\|_{1}
(Lemma
C.3
).
Since
\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}
is a subroutine of
{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}
with correspondence
(1,\ldots,9;1,\ldots,16)
by Definition
C.3
, and since
\operatorname{Sh}_{(1,\ldots,9),(1,\ldots,16)}(c_{2t})=c_{2t}
and
\operatorname{Lift}_{(1,\ldots,9),(1,\ldots,16)}^{\varnothing}(c_{2t+1})=c_{2t+1}
, Lemma
C.1
gives

{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}^{T_{t}}(c_{2t})=c_{2t+1},\quad\|c_{2t}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}}^{T_{t}}\leq\|c_{2t}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{\pi}}}^{T_{t}}\vee\|c_{2t}\|\leq 1.

(b)

Let
t\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
. In configuration
c_{2t+1}
, the machine is in State 9 and reads the symbol
1
, so after one step, the machine is in configuration
c_{2(t+1)}
. Moreover, we have

\|c_{2t+1}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}}^{1}\leq\|c_{2t+1}\|\vee\|c_{2t+2}\|\leq 1.

(c)

In configuration
c_{2d+1}
, the machine is in State 9 and reads the symbol
0
, so after one step, the machine is in configuration
c_{2d+2}
. Moreover, we have

\|c_{2d+1}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}}^{1}\leq\|c_{2d+1}\|\vee\|c_{2d+2}\|\leq 1.

(d)

Define the configurations
c^{\prime}_{2d+2}:=(1;|1^{k_{P}}0v;F_{P^{\ast},n}(x))
, and
c^{\prime}_{2d+3}:=(2;|v;2^{k_{P}}F_{P^{\ast},n}(x))
. By Lemma
C.2
, we have

{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}}(c^{\prime}_{2d+2})=c^{\prime}_{2d+3},\quad\|c^{\prime}_{2d+2}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}}\leq|2^{k_{P}}F_{P^{\ast},n}(x)|=|F_{P,n}(x)|.

Since
{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}
is a subroutine of
{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}
with correspondence
(10,11;2)
by Definition
C.3
, and since

\operatorname{Sh}_{(10,11),(2)}(c_{2d+2})=c^{\prime}_{2d+2},\quad\operatorname{Lift}_{(10,11),(2)}^{(x,0_{14})}(c^{\prime}_{2d+3})=c_{2d+3},

Lemma
C.1
gives

{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}}(c_{2d+2})=c_{2d+3},\quad\|c_{2d+2}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}}\leq\|c^{\prime}_{2d+2}\|_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}^{T_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}}\vee\|c_{2d+2}\|\leq 1\vee|F_{P,n}(x)|.

This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

By invoking repeatedly the Chaining Lemma
C
together with the claim, and using
c=c_{0}
and
c^{\prime}=c_{2d+3}
, we obtain

{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}^{T}(c)=c^{\prime},

where

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{d}T_{t}+d+1+T_{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{d}\len{v^{(b_{t})}}+(d+1)(2n+5)+d+1+k_{P}+1

\displaystyle=\len{u_{P}}+\|P\|_{0}(2n+5).

Moreover,

\|c\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}}^{T}\leq 1\vee|F_{P,n}(x)|.

∎

C.4
Approximation of continuous functions by TMNUs

The final piece is to construct a TMNU that, given the encoding of a sequence
\mathbf{P}:=(P_{i})_{i\in\No}\in\Db[X]
of polynomials with dyadic coefficients, a sequence of natural numbers
\mathbf{n}:=(n_{i})_{i\in\No}
initially written on its tape, and its neural state initially containing some
x\in[-1,1]
, computes successively
F_{P_{0},n_{0}},F_{P_{1},n_{1}},F_{P_{2},n_{2}},\ldots
. More precisely, we encode the sequence
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{n}
on the tape of the machine as follows.

{definition}

Let
\mathbf{P}:=(P_{i})_{i\in\No}\in\Db[X]
be a sequence of polynomials with dyadic coefficients, and
\mathbf{n}:=(n_{i})_{i\in\No}
be a sequence of natural numbers. We define the encoding of
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{n}
by

\langle\mathbf{P},\mathbf{n}\rangle:=u_{P_{0},n_{0}}u_{P_{1},n_{1}}u_{P_{2},n_{2}}\cdots.

For every
k\in\No
, we let
\mathbf{P}_{\geq k}:=(P_{i+k})_{i\in\No}
and
\mathbf{n}_{\geq k}:=(n_{i+k})_{i\in\No}
. Note that

\langle\mathbf{P}_{\geq k},\mathbf{n}_{\geq k}\rangle=u_{P_{k},n_{k}}u_{P_{k+1},n_{k+1}}\cdots.

This TMNU has neural dimension of 17, and uses the 16 first dimensions to simulate the TMNU
{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}
, and the last dimension is designed to store the value of the last computation of
{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}
. The formal definition of such a machine is given as follows.

{definition}

We let
{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}
be the TMNU with neural dimension
17
and 12 states defined by the following procedure.

•

State 1.
Update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow(\omega_{1},1,0_{14},\omega_{2}),

and go to State 2.

•

State 2-11.
Use
{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}
(Def.
C.3
) as subroutine with correspondence
(2,\ldots,11,1;1,\ldots,16)
.

•

State 12.
Halt.

The following lemma describes one full cycle of this infinite polynomial-evaluation machine.

{lemma}

Let
\mathbf{P}:=(P_{i})_{i\in\No}
be a sequence of polynomials,
\mathbf{n}:=(n_{i})_{i\in\No}
be a sequence of natural numbers, and
x\in[-1,1]
. For all
i\in\No
, define the configuration

c_{i}:=\left(1;|\langle\mathbf{P}_{\geq i},\mathbf{n}_{\geq i}\rangle;x,F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x),0_{14},F_{P_{i-2},n_{i-2}}(x)\right),

with the convention that
F_{P_{-1},n_{-1}}(x):=F_{P_{-2},n_{-2}}(x):=0
. Note that, in particular,
c_{0}:=(1;|\langle\mathbf{P},\mathbf{n}\rangle;x,0_{16})
.
Define

T_{i}:=\len{u_{P_{i}}}+\|P_{i}\|_{0}(2n_{i}+5)+1.

(160)

Then,

{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{T_{i}}(c_{i})=c_{i+1},\quad\|c_{i}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{T_{i}}\leq 1\vee\sup_{j\in\No}|F_{P_{j},n_{j}}(x)|.

(161)

In particular, by denoting
N_{i}:=\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}T_{j}
, we have
{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{N_{i}}(c_{0})=c_{i}
, and

{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{\langle\mathbf{P},\mathbf{n}\rangle}x[t]=F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x),\quad\text{for all }t\in\{N_{i}+1,\ldots,N_{i+1}\},\ i\in\No.

(162)

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{P}:=(P_{i})_{i\in\No}
be a sequence of polynomials,
\mathbf{n}:=(n_{i})_{i\in\No}
be a sequence of natural numbers, and
x\in[-1,1]
. For all
i\in\No
, define the configuration

c_{i}:=\left(1;|\langle\mathbf{P}_{\geq i},\mathbf{n}_{\geq i}\rangle;x,F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x),0_{14},F_{P_{i-2},n_{i-2}}(x)\right).

We also define the configuration
\tilde{c}_{i}
by

\tilde{c}_{i}:=\left(2;|\langle\mathbf{P}_{\geq i},\mathbf{n}_{\geq i}\rangle;x,1,0_{14},F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x)\right).

Fix
i\in\No
, and set

S_{i}:=\len{u_{P_{i}}}+\|P_{i}\|_{0}(2n_{i}+5),\quad\text{and}\quad T_{i}:=S_{i}+1.

If
{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}
is in configuration
c_{i}
, then by definition it goes to configuration
\tilde{c}_{i}
in one transition. Therefore,

{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}(c_{i})=\tilde{c}_{i},

and

\displaystyle\|c_{i}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|(x,F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x),0_{14},F_{P_{i-2},n_{i-2}}(x))\|_{\infty}\vee\|(x,1,0_{14},F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x))\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}1\vee|F_{P_{i-2},n_{i-2}}(x)|\vee|F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x)|,

where (a) follows from
|x|\leq 1
.

We now analyze the
{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}
subroutine started from
\tilde{c}_{i}
. Define the configurations
\tilde{c}^{\prime}_{i}
and
c^{\prime}_{i+1}
by

\tilde{c}^{\prime}_{i}:=\left(1;|\langle\mathbf{P}_{\geq i},\mathbf{n}_{\geq i}\rangle;x,1,0_{14}\right),

and

c^{\prime}_{i+1}:=\left(11;|\langle\mathbf{P}_{\geq i+1},\mathbf{n}_{\geq i+1}\rangle;x,F_{P_{i},n_{i}}(x),0_{14}\right).

Note that
\tilde{c}^{\prime}_{i}
can be rewritten as
\tilde{c}^{\prime}_{i}=(1;|u_{P_{i},n_{i}}\langle\mathbf{P}_{\geq i+1},\mathbf{n}_{\geq i+1}\rangle;x,1,0_{14})
. Therefore, by Lemma
C.3
, we have

{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}^{S_{i}}(\tilde{c}^{\prime}_{i})=c^{\prime}_{i+1},\quad\|\tilde{c}^{\prime}_{i}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}}^{S_{i}}\leq 1\vee|F_{P_{i},n_{i}}(x)|.

Since
{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}
is a subroutine of
{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}
with correspondence
(2,\ldots,11,1;1,\ldots,16)
by Definition
C.4
, and since

\operatorname{Sh}_{(2,\ldots,11,1),(1,\ldots,16)}(\tilde{c}_{i})=\tilde{c}^{\prime}_{i},\quad\operatorname{Lift}_{(2,\ldots,11,1),(1,\ldots,16)}^{F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x)}(c^{\prime}_{i+1})=c_{i+1},

Lemma
C.1
yields

{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{S_{i}}(\tilde{c}_{i})=c_{i+1},

and, for all
t\in\{0,\ldots,S_{i}\}
,

\pi_{17}\pi_{\omega}{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{t}(\tilde{c}_{i})=F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x).

(163)

Moreover, by Lemma
C.1
, we have

\|\tilde{c}_{i}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{S_{i}}\leq\|\tilde{c}^{\prime}_{i}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{\Pi}}}}^{S_{i}}\vee\|\tilde{c}_{i}\|\leq 1\vee|F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x)|\vee|F_{P_{i},n_{i}}(x)|.

By the Chaining Lemma
C
, we obtain

{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{T_{i}}(c_{i})=c_{i+1},

and

\|c_{i}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{T_{i}}\leq 1\vee|F_{P_{i-2},n_{i-2}}(x)|\vee|F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x)|\vee|F_{P_{i},n_{i}}(x)|\leq 1\vee\sup_{j\in\No}|F_{P_{j},n_{j}}(x)|.

We now move to the proof of the last statement. For
i\in\No
, define
N_{i}:=\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}T_{j}
. By induction,

{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{N_{i}}(c_{0})=c_{i},\quad\text{for all }i\in\No.

(164)

Now, fix
i\in\No
and
t\in\{N_{i}+1,\ldots,N_{i+1}\}
. Then
s:=t-N_{i}-1
belongs to
\{0,\ldots,S_{i}\}
, and

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{t}(c_{0})

\displaystyle={\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{s}({\bm{\mathsf{C}}}({\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{N_{i}}(c_{0})))\overset{\eqref{eq:traj_config_0:proof:lem:continuous-machine}}{=}{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{s}({\bm{\mathsf{C}}}(c_{i}))\overset{(a)}{=}{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{s}(\tilde{c}_{i}),

where (a) is by
{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}(c_{i})=\tilde{c}_{i}
. Therefore, by Definition
4.3
,

\displaystyle{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{\langle\mathbf{P},\mathbf{n}\rangle}x[t]

\displaystyle=\pi_{17}\pi_{\omega}{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{t}(c_{0})=\pi_{17}\pi_{\omega}{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{s}(\tilde{c}_{i})\overset{(a)}{=}F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x),

where (a) is by (
163
). This concludes the proof of the last statement.
∎

We finally close this section by showing that the TMNU
{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}
can approximate uniformly any continuous function
f:[-1,1]\to\R
, and is uniformly bounded over its computations. This gives the qualitative approximation theorem at the TMNU level.

{theorem}

For every continuous function
f:[-1,1]\to\R
, there exists
u\in\{0,1\}
such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{u}(x)[t]-f(x)\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}=0,

and

(1;|u;x,0_{16})\in\Bc_{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{\infty}\left(1+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}\right).

Proof.

Let
f:[-1,1]\to\R
be a continuous function. By the Weierstrass approximation theorem and density of dyadic numbers in
\R
, there exists a sequence of polynomials with dyadic coefficients
(P_{i})_{i\in\No}\in\Db[X]
such that
\|f-P_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}\leq 2^{-i-1}
for all
i\in\No
. Further, we define
\mathbf{n}:=(n_{i})_{i\in\No}:=(i+\lceil(k_{P_{i}}+\log_{2}\|P_{i}\|_{0})/2\rceil)_{i\in\No}
. Note that

|F_{P_{i},n_{i}}-P_{i}(x)|\overset{(a)}{\leq}\|P_{i}\|_{1}\|P_{i}\|_{0}2^{-2n_{i}-1}\leq\|P_{i}\|_{1}\|P_{i}\|_{0}2^{-2i-k_{P_{i}}-\log_{2}\|P_{i}\|_{0}-1}\leq 2^{-2i-1},

(165)

where (a) follows by Lemma
C.3
. By Lemma
C.4
, we have that for every
i\in\No
and
t\in\{N_{i}+1,\ldots,N_{i+1}\}
,

\displaystyle|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{\langle\mathbf{P},\mathbf{n}\rangle}x[t]-f(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{\langle\mathbf{P},\mathbf{n}\rangle}x[t]-P_{i}(x)|+|P_{i}(x)-f(x)|\leq|F_{P_{i},n_{i}}-P_{i}(x)|+2^{-i-1}\overset{\eqref{eq:approx_poly:proof:thm:continuous-approximation}}{\leq}2^{-i}.

Upon noting that
t\geq N_{i}+1\iff t\in\cup_{j\geq i}\{N_{j}+1,\ldots,N_{j+1}\}
, we get that for every
i\in\No
,
t\geq N_{i}+1
,

|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{\langle\mathbf{P},\mathbf{n}\rangle}x[t]-f(x)|\leq\sup_{j\geq i}2^{-j}=2^{-i},\ \text{for all}\ x\in[-1,1].

For the second statement, set
C_{f}:=1+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}
. First note that, for every
x\in[-1,1]
,

\displaystyle 1\vee\sup_{i\in\No}|F_{P_{i},n_{i}}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq 1\vee\sup_{i\in\No}\left(|F_{P_{i},n_{i}}(x)-P_{i}(x)|+|P_{i}(x)-f(x)|+|f(x)|\right)

\displaystyle\leq 1\vee\sup_{i\in\No}(2^{-2i-1}+2^{-i-1}+|f(x)|)\leq C_{f}.

Let
c_{0}:=(1;|\langle\mathbf{P},\mathbf{n}\rangle;x,0_{16})
. With the notation of Lemma
C.4
, we have
{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{N_{i}}(c_{0})=c_{i}
and
\|c_{i}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{T_{i}}\leq C_{f}
for every
i\in\No
. Hence, for every
t\in\No
, either
t=0
, in which case
\|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{t}(c_{0})\|=\|c_{0}\|\leq 1\leq C_{f}
, or there exist
i\in\No
and
s\in\{1,\ldots,T_{i}\}
such that
t=N_{i}+s
, and then

\|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{t}(c_{0})\|=\|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{s}(c_{i})\|\leq\|c_{i}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{T_{i}}\leq C_{f}.

Therefore,
c_{0}\in\Bc_{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{\infty}(C_{f})
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Note that in the above proof, we do not know a priori the properties sequence of approximating polynomials given by the Weierstrass approximation theorem, therefore, the speed of convergence of the TMNU
{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}
to
f
is not known. In the remainder of this section, we analyze the convergence rate of
{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}
to
f
when
f
is a polynomial with arbitrary real coefficients.
We first extend the definitions given for dyadic polynomials to polynomials with real coefficients. Specifically, given
P:=a_{0}+a_{1}X+a_{2}X^{2}+\ldots+a_{d}X^{d}\in\R[X]
, we define
||P\|_{0}:=d+1
,
\|P\|_{1}:=\sum_{i=0}^{d}|a_{i}|
,
k_{P}:=\lfloor\log_{2}\|P\|_{1}\rfloor+1
and
P^{\ast}:=2^{-k_{P}}P
. Note that
\|P\|_{1}<2^{k_{P}}
, and hence
\|P^{\ast}\|_{1}<1
.

For
a\in[0,1]
and
n\in\No
, we define the dyadic approximation of
a
at precision
n
by

a\hskip-3.5pt\downharpoonright_{n}:=s(a)\frac{\lfloor 2^{n}|a|\rfloor}{2^{n}}\in 2^{-n}\Z\subseteq\Db.

Note that

|a-2^{-n}|<|a\hskip-3.5pt\downharpoonright_{n}|\leq|a|.

(166)

Given
P=a_{0}+a_{1}X+a_{2}X^{2}+\ldots+a_{d}X^{d}\in\R[X]
and
n\in\No
, we define the dyadic approximation of
P
at precision
n
by
P\hskip-3.5pt\downharpoonright_{n}:=\sum_{j=0}^{d}(a_{j})\hskip-3.5pt\downharpoonright_{n}X^{j}\in 2^{-n}\Z[X]
. Note that

\|P\hskip-3.5pt\downharpoonright_{n}-P\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}\leq\|P\|_{1}(d+1)2^{-n},\|P\hskip-3.5pt\downharpoonright_{n}\|_{1}\leq\|P\|_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\|P\hskip-3.5pt\downharpoonright_{n}\|_{0}\leq\|P\|_{0}.

(167)

Before proving polynomial rates, we need a simple bound on the length of the polynomial encoding.

{lemma}

Let
n\in\No
, and
P\in 2^{-n}\Z[X]
be a polynomial of degree
d
. Then, we have

\len{u_{P}}\leq(d+1)(2(n+k_{P})+2)+1+k_{P}.

Proof.

Let
n\in\No
, and
P\in 2^{-n}\Z[X]
be a polynomial of degree
d
. Then,
P^{\ast}=2^{-k_{P}}P\in 2^{-n-k_{P}}\Z[X]\cap\Db_{1}[X]
. Now, for every
a\in 2^{-n-k_{P}}\Z\cap\Db_{1}
, we have
|a|\in 2^{-n-k_{P}}\Z\cap\Db^{+}_{1}
, and hence
\len{u^{(|a|)}}\leq n+k_{P}
, which implies that

\len{v^{(a)}}=2\len{u^{(|a|)}}+2\leq 2(n+k_{P})+2.

(168)

Therefore, by definition of
u_{P}
, we have

\len{u_{P}}=\sum_{j=0}^{d}\len{v^{(b_{j})}}+2(d+1)+1+k_{P}\leq(d+1)(2(n+k_{P})+2)+1+k_{P}.

∎

We now are ready to prove the main result of this section, which gives an upper bound on the convergence rate of
{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}
to a polynomial
P
with real coefficients.

{theorem}

Let
P\in\R[X]
be a polynomial of degree
d
. Then, there exists
u\in\{0,1\}
such that for all
x\in[-1,1]
and
t\geq\tau_{P}
,

\left|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{u}x[t]-P(x)\right|\leq 2\cdot 2^{-\frac{t}{t_{P}}},

where
t_{P}:=18(d+1)
and
\tau_{P}:=t_{P}(6k_{P}+d+11)+1
, and

(1;|u;x,0_{16})\in\Bc_{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{\infty}\left(1+\|P\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}\right),\quad\text{for all }x\in[-1,1].

Proof.

Let
P\in\R[X]
be a polynomial of degree
d
. Define
\mathbf{n}:=(n_{i})_{i\in\No}
by
n_{i}:=2^{i+1}+k_{P}+\lceil\log_{2}(d+1)\rceil+1
and
\mathbf{P}:=(P_{i})_{i\in\No}
by
P_{i}:=P\hskip-3.5pt\downharpoonright_{n_{i}}
. Set
u:=\langle\mathbf{P},\mathbf{n}\rangle
. Note that

\displaystyle|F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x)-P(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x)-P_{i-1}(x)|+|P_{i-1}(x)-P(x)|

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\|P_{i-1}\|_{1}\|P_{i-1}\|_{0}2^{-2n_{i-1}-1}+\|P\|_{1}(d+1)2^{-n_{i-1}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|P\|_{1}(d+1)2^{-n_{i-1}}\leq 2^{-2^{i}}

(169)

for every
i\in\No
and
x\in[-1,1]
, where (a) follows by Lemma
C.3
.
By Lemma
C.4
, there exists a nondecreasing sequence
(N_{i})_{i\in\No}\in
such that

|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{u}x[t]-P(x)|=|F_{P_{i-1},n_{i-1}}(x)-P(x)|\leq 2^{-2^{i}},\quad\text{for all }t\in\{N_{i}+1,\ldots,N_{i+1}\},\ i\in\No,

(170)

which upon noting that
t\geq N_{i}+1\iff t\in\bigcup_{j\geq i}\{N_{j}+1,\ldots,N_{j+1}\}
, delivers that for all
t\geq N_{i}+1
,

|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{u}x[t]-P(x)|\leq\sup_{j\geq i}2^{-2^{j}}=2^{-2^{i}}.

Moreover, Lemma
C.4
also delivers that for all
i\in\No
,

\displaystyle N_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\left(\len{u_{P_{j}}}+\|P_{j}\|_{0}(2n_{j}+5)+1\right)

where by noting that
P_{j}\in 2^{j}\Z
and applying Lemma
C.4
, we get

\len{u_{P_{j}}}\leq(d+1)(2(n_{j}+k_{P_{j}})+2)+1+k_{P_{j}},

and hence, by injecting
n_{j}\leq 2^{j+1}+k_{P}+d+2
, and noting that
k_{P_{j}}\leq k_{P}
and
\|P_{j}\|_{0}\leq d+1
, we get

\displaystyle N_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\left((d+1)\left(4\left(2^{j+1}+k_{P}+d+2\right)+k_{P}+7\right)+2+k_{P}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}(d+1)\left(8\cdot 2^{j}+6k_{P}+d+11\right)\leq 9(d+1)2^{i},

for
i\geq\alpha_{P}:=\log_{2}(6k_{P}+d+11)
.
Now, we let
\tilde{t}_{P}:=9(d+1)
, and note that for every

t\geq\tau_{P}:=\tilde{t}_{P}2^{\alpha_{P}+1}+1=18(d+1)(6k_{P}+d+11)+1,

we have
\lfloor\log_{2}((t-1)/\tilde{t}_{P})\rfloor\geq\lfloor\alpha_{P}+1\rfloor\geq\alpha_{P}
, and hence

N_{\lfloor\log_{2}((t-1)/\tilde{t}_{P})\rfloor}+1\leq\tilde{t}_{P}2^{\lfloor\log_{2}((t-1)/\tilde{t}_{P})\rfloor}+1\leq\tilde{t}_{P}2^{\log_{2}((t-1)/\tilde{t}_{P})}+1=t,

so that, by (
169
),

|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{u}x[t]-P(x)|\leq 2^{-2^{\lfloor\log_{2}((t-1)/\tilde{t}_{P})\rfloor}}\leq 2^{-2^{\log_{2}((t-1)/\tilde{t}_{P})-1}}=2^{-\frac{t-1}{2\tilde{t}_{P}}}=2^{-\frac{t}{t_{P}}}\cdot 2^{\frac{1}{t_{P}}}\leq 2\cdot 2^{-\frac{t}{t_{P}}}.

Finally, set
C_{P}:=1+\|P\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}
and fix
x\in[-1,1]
. By (
169
), applied with
i+1
in place of
i
, we have

\sup_{i\in\No}|F_{P_{i},n_{i}}(x)-P(x)|\leq 1.

Hence,

1\vee\sup_{i\in\No}|F_{P_{i},n_{i}}(x)|\leq 1\vee\left(1+|P(x)|\right)\leq C_{P}.

Let
c_{0}:=(1;|u;x,0_{16})
. With the notation of Lemma
C.4
, we have
{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{N_{i}}(c_{0})=c_{i}
and
\|c_{i}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{T_{i}}\leq C_{P}
for every
i\in\No
. Therefore, for every
t\in\No
, either
t=0
, in which case
\|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{t}(c_{0})\|=\|c_{0}\|\leq 1\leq C_{P}
, or there exist
i\in\No
and
s\in\{1,\ldots,T_{i}\}
such that
t=N_{i}+s
, and then

\|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{t}(c_{0})\|=\|{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{s}(c_{i})\|\leq\|c_{i}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}}^{T_{i}}\leq C_{P}.

This proves that
c_{0}\in\Bc_{\bm{\mathsf{C}}}^{\infty}(C_{P})
.
∎

C.5
Native evaluation of Chebyshev expansions

In this section, we show how to implement a native evaluation of Chebyshev expansions using the TMNU
{\boldsymbol{\times}}
as a subroutine. The main idea is to use the recurrence relation of Chebyshev polynomials to evaluate the expansion without explicitly computing the polynomials themselves. Namely, we use the recurrence relation

T_{0}(x)=1,\quad T_{1}(x)=x,\quad T_{n+1}(x)=2xT_{n}(x)-T_{n-1}(x),\quad n\in\No,

(171)

to compute the Chebyshev polynomials iteratively. This allows us to evaluate the Chebyshev partial sums
S^{(f)}_{n}(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}c^{(f)}_{j}T_{j}(x)
efficiently, using the coefficients
c^{(f)}_{j}
obtained from the Chebyshev series representation of the function
f
.

Before proceeding, we introduce some notation to simplify the presentation.For
n\in\No
, write

\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,y):=\Rc^{\times}\Dc(x,y)[n],\qquad(x,y)\in[-1,1]^{2},

and define the approximate normalized Chebyshev sequence by

Q_{0,n}(x):=\frac{1}{2},\qquad Q_{1,n}(x):=\frac{x}{2},\qquad Q_{k+1,n}(x):=2\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,Q_{k,n}(x))-Q_{k-1,n}(x).

(172)

The normalization by
1/2
leaves room for the numerical error while calling
{\boldsymbol{\times}}
. The next machine implements one step of this normalized recurrence.

{definition}

We let
{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}
be the TMNU with neural dimension
17
and
6
states defined by the following procedure.

•

State 1.
Update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3},\omega_{1},\omega_{3},0_{12}),

and go to State
2
.

•

States 2–5.
Use
{\boldsymbol{\times}}
as a subroutine with correspondence
(2,3,4,5;4,\ldots,17)
.

•

State 5.
Update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow(\omega_{1},\omega_{3},2\omega_{4}-\omega_{2},0_{14}),

and go to State
6
.

•

State 6.
Halt.

We first verify the effect and boundedness of a single Chebyshev step.

{lemma}

Let
n\in\No
,
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
,
x,a,b\in[-1,1]
, and define

\widetilde{b}:=2\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,b)-a.

For the configurations

c:=(1;|1^{n}0v;x,a,b,0_{14}),\qquad c^{\prime}:=(6;|v;x,b,\widetilde{b},0_{14}),

and
T:=2n+5
, we have

{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}^{T}(c)=c^{\prime},\qquad\|c\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}}^{T}\leq 3.

Proof.

Let

T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}:=2n+3,\qquad T:=T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}+2=2n+5.

We define the following four configurations of
{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}
:

\begin{cases}c_{0}&:=(1;|1^{n}0v;x,a,b,0_{14}),\\
c_{1}&:=(2;|1^{n}0v;x,a,b,x,b,0_{12}),\\
c_{2}&:=(5;|v;x,a,b,\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,b),0_{13}),\\
c_{3}&:=(6;|v;x,b,2\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,b)-a,0_{14}).\end{cases}

(173)

Note that
c_{0}=c
and
c_{3}=c^{\prime}
. We prove separately the three stages of the computation.

{claim*}

The following statements hold:

(a)

{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}(c_{0})=c_{1}
and
\|c_{0}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}}^{1}\leq 1
.

(b)

{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}(c_{1})=c_{2}
and

\|c_{1}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}\leq 1
.

(c)

{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}(c_{2})=c_{3}
and

\|c_{2}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}}^{1}\leq 3
.

Proof of the Claim.

We prove each statement in turn.

(a)

In configuration
c_{0}
, the machine is in State
1
. Therefore, by Definition
C.5
, it preserves the first three neural coordinates, copies
x
and
b
into coordinates
4
and
5
, sets all remaining coordinates to zero, and goes to State
2
. Hence,

{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}(c_{0})=c_{1}.

Moreover, since
x,a,b\in[-1,1]
,

\|c_{0}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}}^{1}\leq\|(x,a,b,0_{14})\|_{\infty}\vee\|(x,a,b,x,b,0_{12})\|_{\infty}\leq 1.

(b)

Define the configurations of
{\boldsymbol{\times}}

c_{1}^{\prime}:=(1;|1^{n}0v;x,b,0_{12}),\qquad c_{2}^{\prime}:=(4;|v;\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,b),0_{13}).

By Lemma
C.2
,

{\boldsymbol{\times}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}(c_{1}^{\prime})=c_{2}^{\prime},\qquad\|c_{1}^{\prime}\|_{\boldsymbol{\times}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}\leq 1.

By Definition
C.5
,
{\boldsymbol{\times}}
is a subroutine of
{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}
with correspondence

(2,3,4,5;4,\ldots,17)
. Furthermore,

\operatorname{Sh}_{(2,3,4,5),(4,\ldots,17)}(c_{1})=c_{1}^{\prime},

and

\operatorname{Lift}_{(2,3,4,5),(4,\ldots,17)}^{(x,a,b)}(c_{2}^{\prime})=c_{2}.

Therefore, Lemma
C.1
yields

{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}(c_{1})=\operatorname{Lift}_{(2,3,4,5),(4,\ldots,17)}^{(x,a,b)}\left({\boldsymbol{\times}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}(c_{1}^{\prime})\right)=c_{2},

and

\|c_{1}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}\leq\|c_{1}^{\prime}\|_{\boldsymbol{\times}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}\vee\|c_{1}\|\leq 1.

(c)

In configuration
c_{2}
, the machine is in State
5
. By Definition
C.5
, it preserves
x
, moves
b
into the second coordinate, places

2\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,b)-a
in the third coordinate, sets the remaining coordinates to zero, and goes to State
6
. Thus,

{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}(c_{2})=c_{3}.

Lemma
C.2
gives

|\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,b)|\leq 1
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\|c_{2}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}}^{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|c_{2}\|\vee\|c_{3}\|

\displaystyle\leq 1\vee\left|2\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,b)-a\right|

\displaystyle\leq 3.

This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

By repeatedly applying the Chaining Lemma
C
to the three stages of the claim, we obtain

{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}^{T}(c)=c^{\prime},

where
T=1+T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}+1=2n+5
. Moreover,

\|c\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}}^{T}\leq\|c_{0}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}}^{1}\vee\|c_{1}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}}^{T_{\boldsymbol{\times}}}\vee\|c_{2}\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}}^{1}\leq 3.

∎

The next estimate translates the approximate multiplication error into a recurrence error.

{lemma}

Let
n\in\No
,
k\in
,
x,a,b\in[-1,1]
, and define

\widetilde{b}:=2\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,b)-a.

Define also the incoming errors

E_{-}:=a-\frac{T_{k-1}(x)}{2},\qquad E:=b-\frac{T_{k}(x)}{2},

and the local multiplication error

\varepsilon_{n}(x,b):=\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,b)-xb.

Then,

\widetilde{b}-\frac{T_{k+1}(x)}{2}=2xE-E_{-}+2\varepsilon_{n}(x,b),\qquad|\varepsilon_{n}(x,b)|\leq 2^{-2n-1}.

(174)

Proof.

By definition,

\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,b)=xb+\varepsilon_{n}(x,b).

Since
x,b\in[-1,1]
, Lemma
C.2
gives

|\varepsilon_{n}(x,b)|=|\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,b)-xb|\leq 2^{-2n-1}.

Using

a=\frac{T_{k-1}(x)}{2}+E_{-},\qquad b=\frac{T_{k}(x)}{2}+E,

we obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{b}

\displaystyle=2\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,b)-a

\displaystyle=2(xb+\varepsilon_{n}(x,b))-\left(\frac{T_{k-1}(x)}{2}+E_{-}\right)

\displaystyle=2x\left(\frac{T_{k}(x)}{2}+E\right)+2\varepsilon_{n}(x,b)-\left(\frac{T_{k-1}(x)}{2}+E_{-}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2xT_{k}(x)-T_{k-1}(x)}{2}+2xE-E_{-}+2\varepsilon_{n}(x,b)

\displaystyle=\frac{T_{k+1}(x)}{2}+2xE-E_{-}+2\varepsilon_{n}(x,b).

Rearranging proves (
174
).
∎

Iterating the previous recurrence gives a uniform stability bound for the normalized Chebyshev values. The key is to remark that the error propagation follows the same recurrence as the Chebyshev polynomials of the second kind, defined by the recurrence

U_{0}(x)=1,\qquad U_{1}(x)=2x,\qquad U_{r+1}(x)=2xU_{r}(x)-U_{r-1}(x),\quad r\in,

(175)

for
x\in[-1,1]
. We show the following classical boundedness property of the Chebyshev polynomials of the second kind.

{lemma}

For
r\in
,

\sup_{x\in[-1,1]}|U_{r}(x)|=r+1.

Proof.

The proof follows by first showing a useful formula for
U_{r}(x)
, and then using it to find the maximum value of
|U_{r}(x)|
on the interval
[-1,1]
. We show directly from the recurrence that, for every
\theta\in[0,\pi]
,

U_{r}(\cos(\theta))=\sum_{\ell=0}^{r}e^{i(r-2\ell)\theta}.

For
r=0
, this says
U_{0}(\cos(\theta))=1
, which is true. For

r=1
, this says

U_{1}(\cos(\theta))=e^{i\theta}+e^{-i\theta}=2\cos(\theta),

which is also true. For the inductive step, assume the formula holds at
the two consecutive indices
r
and
r-1
. Then the recurrence
(
175
) gives

\displaystyle U_{r+1}(\cos(\theta))

\displaystyle=2\cos(\theta)U_{r}(\cos(\theta))-U_{r-1}(\cos(\theta))

\displaystyle=(e^{i\theta}+e^{-i\theta})\sum_{\ell=0}^{r}e^{i(r-2\ell)\theta}-\sum_{\ell=0}^{r-1}e^{i(r-1-2\ell)\theta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{r}e^{i(r+1-2\ell)\theta}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{r}e^{i(r-1-2\ell)\theta}-\sum_{\ell=0}^{r-1}e^{i(r-1-2\ell)\theta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{r}e^{i(r+1-2\ell)\theta}+e^{-i(r+1)\theta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{r+1}e^{i(r+1-2\ell)\theta}.

This completes the induction. Now, now that

\sup_{x\in[-1,1]}|U_{r}(x)|=\sup_{\theta\in[0,\pi]}|U_{r}(\cos(\theta))|=\sup_{\theta\in[0,\pi]}\left|\sum_{\ell=0}^{r}e^{i(r-2\ell)\theta}\right|\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{r}|e^{i(r-2\ell)\theta}|=r+1.

∎

We are now ready to prove the stability of the normalized Chebyshev sequence.

{lemma}

Let
k,n\in\No
satisfy

k\leq 2^{n}.

(176)

Then,

|Q_{k,n}(x)|\leq 1,\qquad\left|Q_{k,n}(x)-\frac{T_{k}(x)}{2}\right|\leq k^{2}2^{-2n-1},\quad x\in[-1,1].

Proof.

Fix
n\in\No
.
For every
k\in\No
, define

E_{k,n}(x):=Q_{k,n}(x)-\frac{T_{k}(x)}{2},

and the local multiplication error

\varepsilon_{k,n}(x):=\operatorname{Mult}_{n}(x,Q_{k,n}(x))-xQ_{k,n}(x).

We claim that, for every
k\in\{0,\ldots,2^{n}\}
,

|Q_{k,n}(x)|\leq 1,\qquad E_{k,n}(x)=2\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}U_{k-j-1}(x)\varepsilon_{j,n}(x),

(177)

for
x\in[-1,1]
, where
U_{r},r\in\No
are the Chebyshev polynomials of the second kind defined by (
175
). We prove the claim by induction on
k
. For
k=0,1
, the sum is empty, and (
171
) and (
172
) yield
E_{0,n}(x)=E_{1,n}(x)=0
and
|Q_{0,n}(x)|=|Q_{1,n}(x)|\leq 1/2
. This verifies the claim at the base cases.
Now assume that (
177
) holds at the two consecutive indices
k-1
and

k
such that
k<2^{n}
. Then, by Lemma
C.5
,

\displaystyle E_{k+1,n}(x)

\displaystyle=2xE_{k,n}(x)-E_{k-1,n}(x)+2\varepsilon_{k,n}(x)

\displaystyle=4x\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}U_{k-j-1}(x)\varepsilon_{j,n}(x)-2\sum_{j=1}^{k-2}U_{k-j-2}(x)\varepsilon_{j,n}(x)+2\varepsilon_{k,n}(x)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=1}^{k-2}\left(2xU_{k-j-1}(x)-U_{k-j-2}(x)\right)\varepsilon_{j,n}(x)+4xU_{0}(x)\varepsilon_{k-1,n}(x)+2\varepsilon_{k,n}(x)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=1}^{k-2}U_{k-j}(x)\varepsilon_{j,n}(x)+2U_{1}(x)\varepsilon_{k-1,n}(x)+2U_{0}(x)\varepsilon_{k,n}(x)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=1}^{k}U_{k-j}(x)\varepsilon_{j,n}(x).

Now, since by Lemma
C.5
,
|U_{r}(x)|\leq r+1
on
[-1,1]
, we have

|E_{k,n}(x)|\leq 2^{-2n}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}(k-j)\leq k^{2}2^{-2n-1}.

Moreover, since
|T_{k}(x)|\leq 1
on
[-1,1]
,

|Q_{k,n}(x)|\leq\left|\frac{T_{k}(x)}{2}\right|+|E_{k,n}(x)|\leq\frac{1}{2}+k^{2}2^{-2n-1}\overset{(a)}{\leq}\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}=1,

where (a) is by the assumption
k\leq 2^{n}
. This closes the induction and proves the claim. Finally, the error bound follows from the claim and the bound on
|U_{r}(x)|
.
∎

For a dyadic vector
\mathbf{a}=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d})\in\Db^{d+1}
, define

H_{\mathbf{a},n}(x):=2\sum_{k=0}^{d}a_{k}Q_{k,n}(x).

(178)

If
\mathbf{a}\neq 0
, let

k_{\mathbf{a}}:=1+\left(\left\lfloor\log_{2}(2\|\mathbf{a}\|_{1})\right\rfloor\vee 0\right),\qquad\mathbf{a}^{\ast}:=2^{-k_{\mathbf{a}}}\mathbf{a},

(179)

and set
k_{\mathbf{0}}:=0
and
\mathbf{0}^{\ast}:=\mathbf{0}
. Thus,

2\|\mathbf{a}^{\ast}\|_{1}<1
. Define the encoding

\displaystyle u^{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathbf{a},n}

\displaystyle:=v^{(a_{0}^{\ast})}1^{n}0\,1\,v^{(a_{1}^{\ast})}1^{n}0\,1\cdots 1\,v^{(a_{d}^{\ast})}1^{n}0\,0\,1^{k_{\mathbf{a}}}0.

The summation machine now loops over the encoded coefficients and calls
{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}
after each accumulation.

{definition}

We let
{\bm{\mathsf{TSum}}}
be the TMNU with neural dimension
18
and
13
states defined by the following procedure.

•

State 1.
Update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3},\omega_{4},2\omega_{2},0_{13}),

and go to State
2
.

•

States 2–4.
Use
{\bm{\mathsf{Hom}}}
as a subroutine with correspondence
(2,3,4,5;5,6)
.

•

State 5.
Update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3},\omega_{4}+\omega_{5},0_{14}),

and go to State
6
.

•

States 6–10.
Use
{\bm{\mathsf{TStep}}}
as a subroutine with correspondence

(6,\ldots,11;1,2,3,5,\ldots,18)
.

•

State 11.
If the scanned symbol is
1
, erase it, move right, and go to State
1
. If it is
0
, erase it, move right, update the neural state as

\omega\leftarrow(\omega_{1},\omega_{4},0_{16}),

and go to State
12
.

•

State 12.
Use
{\bm{\mathsf{S}}}
as a subroutine with correspondence
(12,13;2)
.

•

State 13.
Halt.

The next lemma verifies one complete encoded Chebyshev-sum evaluation.

{lemma}

Let
\mathbf{a}=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d})\in\Db^{d+1}
,
n\in\No
such that
d+1\leq 2^{n}
,
v\in\{0,1\}^{\#}
, and
x\in[-1,1]
. Define

T_{\mathbf{a},n}:=\sum_{k=0}^{d}\left(\len{v^{(a_{k}^{\ast})}}+2n+8\right)+k_{\mathbf{a}}+1.

(180)

For

c:=(1;|u^{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathbf{a},n}v;x,\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{x}{2},0,0_{14}),\qquad c^{\prime}:=(13;|v;x,H_{\mathbf{a},n}(x),0_{16}),

we have

{\bm{\mathsf{TSum}}}^{T_{\mathbf{a},n}}(c)=c^{\prime},\qquad\|c\|_{{\bm{\mathsf{TSum}}}}^{T_{\mathbf{a},n}}\leq 3\vee|H_{\mathbf{a},n}(x)|.

Proof.

For every
k\in\{0,\ldots,d+1\}
, define
